U_{r}=\left(2\overset{s}{\underset{p^{*}=1}{\sum}}\left[1+\lceil\frac{cc^{\prime}c^{\prime\prime}2s|\det(T_{k})|\textsf{K}}{c_{1}}\rceil\cdot l^{2\alpha_{0}}\right]\right)^{n}
for suitable constants.

In summary, our starting point was to consider the level-
l
pieces, identified by the vectors
\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}T_{k,p}^{-i}(d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}T_{k,p}^{-i}(d^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}
, (
p=1,2...,s
), which intersect an n-cube
C
of side length
c_{0}m^{-r}

and commensurable with
C
. We have fixed
\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}T_{k,p}^{-i}(d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}

at the beginning of the proof, and then obtained upper bounds
for the norms of the vectors

\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}(T_{k,p})^{l_{p}-i}(d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}-d^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}
.
This means that the vectors
\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}(T_{k,p})^{l_{p}-i}(d^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}

fall into a sphere with center
\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}(T_{k,p})^{l_{p}-i}(d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}

and fixed radius given by (
3.46
), or into an
n
-cube with side length the diameter of the sphere. But these vectors are integer vectors. Therefore,
the number of the integer vectors
\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}(T_{k,p})^{l_{p}-i}(d^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}=T_{k,p}^{l_{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}(T_{k,p})^{-i}(d^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}

corresponding to
C
must be bounded. It follows that the number of the vectors
\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}(T_{k,p})^{-i}(d^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}

intersecting
C
is also bounded
by a natural number
U_{r}
as stated in the lemma.

As for
\tilde{F}=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
, consider the level-
l
pieces, identified by the vectors
\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}J_{k,{p^{*}}}^{-i}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}})_{p^{*}}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}J_{k,{p^{*}}}^{-i}(\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}})_{p^{*}}
. Assume that they intersect an n-cube
C
of side length
c_{0}m^{-r}

and commensurable with
C
.
Similar to (
3.46
), we get

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}((J_{k})^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}-i}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}-\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}))_{p^{*}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}J_{k,{p^{*}}}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}-i}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}-\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}})_{p^{*}}\right|

(3.47)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle cc^{\prime\prime\prime}\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}\frac{c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}2sr_{1}r_{2}\cdots r_{s}\textsf{K}}{c_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle cc^{\prime\prime\prime}\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}\frac{c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}2s|\det(J_{k})|\textsf{K}}{c_{1}},

where

\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}},\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}\in\tilde{A}_{k}
and
c^{\prime\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime},\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{N}

are constants. However, this time,
\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}J_{k,{p^{*}}}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}-i}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}-\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}})_{p^{*}}
may not be integer vectors. To overcome this difficulty, we pass to integer vector. For that, we use the integral self-affine set
F=F(T^{k},A_{k})
and the identity

P^{-1}T^{k}=J_{k}P^{-1}
so that
P^{-1}F=\tilde{F}=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
.
This yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{||P||}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}((T^{k})^{{l_{p^{*}}}-i}(a_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}-a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}))_{p^{*}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}(P^{-1}(T^{k})^{{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}-i}(a_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}-a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}))_{p^{*}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}((J_{k})^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}-i}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}-\tilde{a}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}))_{p^{*}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle cc^{\prime\prime\prime}\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}\frac{c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}2s|\det(J_{k})|\textsf{K}}{c_{1}}

for each
p^{*}\in\{1,2,...,s\}
by (
3.47
).
Thus
\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}((T^{k})^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}-i}(a_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}-a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}))_{p^{*}}
are integer vectors. Then we fix
\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}((T^{k})^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}-i}a_{\mathbf{j}_{i}})_{p^{*}}
, let
\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}}((T^{k})^{\tilde{l}_{p^{*}}-i}a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}))_{p^{*}}
vary and reach the same conclusion as above.

The last claim of the lemma follows from the last assertion of Lemma
2.5
.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.6
.

(i) We will see that the dependence on
l
in the above lemma has no affect to our proofs so that one may also assume that
U_{r}
is a constant without loss of generality.

(ii) In the actual case, i.e., if we don’t assume the sloppy notation for pieces, and if we consider all pieces
P_{l}(F_{k})
which are commensurable with a single cube and intersect that cube, then the upper bound in the lemma takes the form
\alpha_{3}2^{\alpha_{1}l^{\alpha_{2}}}
(
\alpha_{3}
is a positive constant). Because in Definition
3.1
-(ii),
we have observed that every piece is between two (cylinder) sets in
F_{k}
. That is, if we set
\mathbf{I}_{1}=\mathbf{j}_{1}...\mathbf{j}_{l}
(with
l=l_{1}
) and
\mathbf{I}_{s}=\mathbf{j}_{1}...\mathbf{j}_{l_{s}}
, then

(F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{l_{1}}T_{k}^{-i}d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}+T_{k}^{-l_{1}}F_{k}\subseteq P_{l}(F_{k})\subseteq\sum_{i=1}^{l_{s}}T_{k}^{-i}d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}+T_{k}^{-l_{s}}F_{k}=(F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{s}},

and
P_{l}(F_{k})
is a finite union of the cylinder sets
(F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}

for certain
d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}\in D_{k}
(
1\leq i\leq l_{1}=l
), but
d_{{\mathbf{j}_{1}}},...,d_{{\mathbf{j}_{l_{s}}}}
are fixed. Let
m_{1}=\lceil|\lambda_{1}|^{k}\rceil
as in Definition
3.1
. Note that for the cylinder sets in the above inclusions, we also have

c^{\prime}l^{-\alpha_{0}}(m_{1})^{-l}\leq diam((F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}})\leq\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\leq c^{\prime\prime}l^{\alpha_{0}}(m_{1})^{-l}

(3.48)

for suitable
constants
c^{\prime}(k),\ c^{\prime\prime}(k),\ \alpha_{0}>0
by the proof of Lemma
3.4
(with
J_{k}
replaced by
T_{k}
and
|\lambda_{1}|^{k}
replaced by
m_{1}
). That yields

diam((F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}})\leq diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\leq c^{\prime\prime}l^{\alpha_{0}}(m_{1})^{-l}\leq\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{c^{\prime}}l^{2\alpha_{0}}diam((F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}).

Also for cylinder sets, the above proof certainly holds.
If a cylinder set
(F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}
is commensurable with a cube
C
as in Lemma
3.5
, then the number of all possible unions of such cylinder sets is bounded by a number in the form of
\alpha_{3}2^{((\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{c^{\prime}})^{n}\alpha_{1}l^{\alpha_{2}+2n\alpha_{0}})}
by the same lemma. Absorbing
2n\alpha_{0}
into
\alpha_{2}
and

(\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{c^{\prime}})^{n}
into
\alpha_{1}
, we get
\alpha_{3}2^{\alpha_{1}l^{\alpha_{2}}}
. With this more general upper bound, our proofs will hold too.
\Box

4
Neighbor Structure of Dynamical Perturbations

In this section, we will see that overlap structures of an integral self-affine set
F=F(T,A)
and its perturbations
F_{k}
are closely related. To compare the overlap structures
F
and
F_{k}
, the concept of neighboring pieces is needed (see
[
3
]
).
As in the introduction section, let
f_{\mathbf{i}},f_{\mathbf{j}}
denote the same-length compositions of the IFS maps in (
1.2
) defining
F
. Let
A_{k}
be as in (
1.3
).
For the multi-indices
\mathbf{i}
,
\mathbf{j}
of the same length
|\mathbf{i}|=|\mathbf{j}|=\textsf{r}
, let

F_{\mathbf{i}}:=f_{\mathbf{i}}(F)=T^{-k}(F+a_{\mathbf{i}}),\ \ \quad F_{\mathbf{j}}:=f_{\mathbf{j}}(F)=T^{-k}(F+a_{\mathbf{j}}),\quad a_{\mathbf{i}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}}\in A_{\textsf{r}}

be the corresponding subsets of
F
, sometimes called the cylinder sets. In the definition of
F_{\mathbf{i}}
,
F
may be replaced by other sets.

Definition 4.1
.

F_{\mathbf{i}}
and
F_{\mathbf{j}}
are called “
neighboring pieces of
F
” or
neighbors
if
F_{\mathbf{i}}\cap F_{\mathbf{j}}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathbf{i}\neq\mathbf{j}
.
\hfill\Box

When
\textsf{r}=l
, the sets
F_{\mathbf{i}}
are not necessarily level-
l
pieces
P_{l}(F)
in Definition
3.1
. However, a level-
l
piece is a finite union of the sets
F_{\mathbf{i}}
with
|\mathbf{i}|=l
. Further, every set
F_{\mathbf{i}}
coincides with some piece
P_{l}(F)
when the expansive matrix
T
is a similarity (or all of its eigenvalues have the same modulus) and
\textsf{r}=l
.
All neighboring pieces can be determined by a graph called the “
neighbor graph
” of
F
, where the vertices are certain translation functions denoted by

h(x)=f_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}f_{\mathbf{j}}(x)=x+a_{\mathbf{j}}-a_{\mathbf{i}},\ \ \ \ a_{\mathbf{i}},a_{\mathbf{j}}\in A_{\textsf{r}},\quad\quad F_{\mathbf{i}}\cap F_{\mathbf{j}}\neq\emptyset

(with the root vertex is the identity function) and the multi-indices
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}
are edge labels of finite paths in
[
3
]
.

First, we extract the following lemma from
[
3
, p.134]
.
Let
\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j}_{1}
denote a multi-index obtained by concatenating the multi-indices

\mathbf{j}
and
\mathbf{j}_{1}
(not necessarily of the same length, and we allow
\mathbf{j}
or
\mathbf{j}_{1}
to have length
0
, i.e.
\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j}_{1}=\mathbf{j}_{1}
or
\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j}_{1}=\mathbf{j}
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
F
be an integral self-affine set. Then

(a)

If
F_{\mathbf{i}}\cap F_{\mathbf{j}}\neq\emptyset
, then
|h(0)=a_{\mathbf{j}}-a_{\mathbf{i}}|\leq diam(F).

(b)

If
F_{\mathbf{i}}\cap F_{\mathbf{j}}=\emptyset
, then
\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|a_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j}_{1}}-a_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i}_{1}}|\rightarrow\infty

(
|\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j}_{1}|=|\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i}_{1}|=l
).

Here we would like to present some of the relationships between the neighbor graphs of
\tilde{F}
and its lower perturbation
F_{k}=F(T_{k},D_{k})
.
Let
G_{\tilde{F}}=(V_{\tilde{F}},E_{\tilde{F}})
and
G_{k}=(V_{F_{k}},E_{k})
denote the neighbor graphs of
\tilde{F}=F(J,P^{-1}A)
and
F_{k}
respectively.

V_{\tilde{F}}
,
E_{\tilde{F}}
refer to vertex set and edge (label) set respectively. We will exclude the trivial case
E_{\tilde{F}}=\emptyset
from our consideration.
We do not follow the notation of
[
3
]
, where an edge label is denoted by
(i,j)
or

i,j
and a path label by
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}
. Instead, we use square brackets.
An edge label is in the form of

e=[i,j]
and a path label is in the form of
[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]
(this notation shouldn’t be confused with concatenation
\mathbf{i}\mathbf{j}
!).
If
\mathbf{i}=i_{1}...i_{\textsf{r}},\ \mathbf{j}=j_{1}...j_{\textsf{r}}
, then our
r
-path is
[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]=[i_{1}...i_{\textsf{r}},j_{1}...j_{\textsf{r}}]
so that
[i_{1},j_{1}]
is the first edge and

[i_{\textsf{r}},j_{\textsf{r}}]
is the last edge in this path.
Let
E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
denote the set of sequences of
r
edges,
or shortly the set of
r
-paths in
G_{\tilde{F}}
. Here we should be careful.
\tilde{F}=F(J,P^{-1}A)=F(J,\tilde{A})=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, but the edges of
G_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}
are
k
-paths in

G_{F(J,\tilde{A})}
.
Similarly, we use the notation
E_{k}^{\textsf{r}}
for the set of
r
-paths in
G_{k}
.
From (
2.10
),
we remember the special correspondence

x=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}J_{k}^{-i}\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}=(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ x_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}T_{k}^{-i}d_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}=(d_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}),

(4.49)

where
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}\in\tilde{A}_{k}
and
d_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}\in D_{k}
.

There may be various ways to prove some relations between
G_{\tilde{F}}
and
G_{k}
.
The following shows that there is close relationship between the edges and paths of
G_{k}
and those of
G_{\tilde{F}}.

First, we introduce some notation. For a given
r
, let
E_{k}(\textsf{r})
denote the set of restrictions of the edge labels of
E_{k}
to the first
r
indices . Similarly,
\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})
denote the set of restrictions of the edge labels of
E_{k}
to the last
r
indices.
For example, let
\mathbf{i}_{1}=\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\quad\mathbf{j}_{1}=\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}
and
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E_{k}
. If
|\mathbf{i}_{0}|=|\mathbf{j}_{0}|=\textsf{r}
, then
[\mathbf{i}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{0}]\in E_{k}(\textsf{r})
; if
|\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}|=|\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}|=\textsf{r}
, then
[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r}).

Lastly, let
\mathbf{i}_{2}=\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}},\mathbf{j}_{2}=\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}
with
[\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in E_{k}
, and let
E_{k}^{2}(\textsf{r})
denote the set of restrictions of
2
-paths
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in E_{k}^{2}
to

[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}\mathbf{j}]
when
|\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}|=|\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}|=|\mathbf{i}|=|\mathbf{j}|=\textsf{r}.
Thus
E_{k}^{2}(\textsf{r})
consists of concatenations of certain elements
of
\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})
and
E_{k}(\textsf{r})
respectively.

We also denote the set of multi-indices of length
r
by
W_{\textsf{r}}=\{\mathbf{i}:|\mathbf{i}|=\textsf{r}\}
and the set of infinite sequences of indices by
W_{\infty}=\{\mathbf{i}=i_{1}i_{2}...i_{l}...\ :\ i_{l}=1,2,..,q\}.

Let
W=\bigcup_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\infty}W_{\textsf{r}}
be the set of all multi-indices. Then define a metric
d
on
W\cup W_{\infty}
by
d(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=0\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{i}=\mathbf{j}
,
d(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=d(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{i})
and

d(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\hbox{\ if $\mathbf{i}=\mathbf{j}$;}\\
1/2^{\textsf{r}+1}&\hbox{\ else if $\mathbf{i}\in W_{\textsf{r}},\ \mathbf{j}\in W_{\textsf{s}}$ ($\textsf{r}<\textsf{s}\leq\infty$) \ and \ $i_{1}=j_{1},\ i_{2}=j_{2},...,i_{\textsf{r}}=j_{\textsf{r}}$;}\\
1/2^{l}&\footnotesize{\hbox{\ otherwise $\mathbf{i}\in W_{\textsf{r}},\ \mathbf{j}\in W_{\textsf{s}}$ ($\textsf{r}\leq\textsf{s}\leq\infty$) and $l\ (\leq\textsf{r})$ is the minimum
index
with
$i_{l}\neq j_{l}.$}}\end{array}\right.

Consider
E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
,
E_{k}
,
\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})
and
E_{k}(\textsf{r})
as defined above. Then we have the following results.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\tilde{F}=F(J,\tilde{A})
and
F_{k}
be its perturbations.
For any given positive integer
r
, there exists an integer
k_{\textsf{r}}\geq\textsf{r}
such that

E_{k}(\textsf{r})=E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
.
Consequently, in the Hausdorff metric induced by
d
,
E_{k}(\textsf{r})
converges to the infinite paths of
G_{\tilde{F}}
as
r
goes to infinity
(hence
k\geq\textsf{r}\Rightarrow k\rightarrow\infty
too).

Proof.
We will use Lemma
4.2
to prove the properties of the edges of
F_{k}
stated in the proposition. Similar to (
1.3
), we introduce new notation for multi-index concatenation:

\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{j}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{j}_{\textsf{s}},\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{i}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{i}_{\textsf{s}},

d_{\mathbf{J}}:=\Sigma_{i=1}^{\textsf{s}}T_{k}^{(\textsf{s}-i)}d_{\mathbf{j}_{i}},\quad\quad\quad\quad d_{\mathbf{I}}:=\Sigma_{i=1}^{\textsf{s}}T_{k}^{(\textsf{s}-i)}d_{\mathbf{i}_{i}}\quad\quad d_{\mathbf{i}_{i}},d_{\mathbf{j}_{i}}\in D_{k}.

(4.50)

Thus
\mathbf{J}
and
\mathbf{I}
are obtained by concatenation of
l
blocks (multi-indices), each of which has length
k
.

It is well known that the neighbor graph of an integral self-affine set is finite, i.e. it is finite-type
[
3
]
.
Since
F
and
{F_{k}}
are integral self affine sets, the graphs
G_{\tilde{F}}
and

G_{k}
are finite. By Proposition
2.8
, we have
F_{k}\longrightarrow\tilde{F}
. Then the cardinalities of the vertex sets
V_{\tilde{F}},V_{k}
are bounded above by a positive integer
\textsf{r}_{0}
independent of
k
. To include all cyclic paths, we may choose
\textsf{r}_{0}
to be the minimum of such bounds plus
1
. Thus all edges in
E_{\tilde{F}}
(or all neighbor types in the terminology of
[
3
]
) will appear in the paths of
E_{\tilde{F}}^{\textsf{r}_{0}}
.
Besides, the periods of all cyclic paths will
be bounded by
\textsf{r}_{0}.
This will be used below.

For large
k
, initial parts of edge labels of
G_{k}
coincides with some finite paths of
G_{\tilde{F}}
:

To show that each
r
-label
[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]\notin E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
cannot be the initial part of an edge in
E_{k}
for large
k
,
consider
[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]\notin E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
, where
|\mathbf{i}|=|\mathbf{j}|=r
and the longer multi-indices
\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}},\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}
with any length
|\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}|=|\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}|=k\geq\textsf{r}.

Since
\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}}\cap\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}}=\emptyset
, by Lemma
4.2
-(b) we have

|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}|>diam(\tilde{F})+1+n

(
n
is the space dimension) for all sufficiently large

k\geq\textsf{r}
.
By the definition in (
1.4
), we have
d_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}\rfloor,\ \ d_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}\rfloor
assuming that
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}},\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}\geq 0.
This in turn gives

n>|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}|-|d_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}-d_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}|\Longrightarrow|d_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}-d_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}|>|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}|-n>diam(\tilde{F})+1

for all large
k
.
Since
F_{k}\longrightarrow\tilde{F}
, it follows that
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}diam(F_{k})=diam(\tilde{F})
and hence, there exists a positive integer
k_{\textsf{r}}\geq\textsf{r}
such that for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
, we have
|diam(F_{k})-diam(\tilde{F})|<1
. We finally obtain

|d_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}-d_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}|>diam(F_{k})

for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
. Then, by Lemma
4.2
-(a),
(F_{k})_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}}\cap(F_{k})_{\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}}=\emptyset
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
. That is,
[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]\notin E_{k}(\textsf{r})
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
.
It follows that
E_{k}(\textsf{r})\subseteq E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}\geq\textsf{r}.

Conversely, assume that

[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]\in E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}.

We want to prove the inclusion
E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}\subseteq E_{k}(\textsf{r})
for large
k
. This part requires more delicate study.
Since
\tilde{F}
is finite-type (i.e.,
G_{\tilde{F}}
is a finite graph), its graph always contains eventually cyclic paths. As mentioned above, the periods of such cycles are bounded above by an integer. Let
p
denote the least common multiple of all such periods. Then from the neighbor graph of
\tilde{F}
, the
r
-path
[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]
can be extended to eventually periodic infinite paths in the form of

[\mathbf{I},\mathbf{J}]=[\mathbf{i}_{1}\overline{\mathbf{i}_{2}},\mathbf{j}_{1}\overline{\mathbf{j}_{2}}]

with
\mathbf{i}_{1}=\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i^{\prime}}
,
\mathbf{j}_{1}=\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j^{\prime}}
,
|\mathbf{i}_{1}|=|\mathbf{j}_{1}|=k
,
|\mathbf{i}_{2}|=|\mathbf{j}_{2}|=k\textsf{p}
and repeating blocks
\mathbf{i}_{2}
and
\mathbf{j}_{2}
. Here we chose
k
such that
k-\textsf{r}\geq{\textsf{r}}_{0}
so as to make last index of
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]
is an edge label of a cyclic path (keeping in mind that the period of any such path divides
p
) and thus we can give the repeating blocks
\mathbf{i}_{2}
and
\mathbf{j}_{2}
. Let
l
be the number of occurrences of
\mathbf{i}_{2}
,
\mathbf{j}_{2}
in
\overline{\mathbf{i}_{2}},\ \overline{\mathbf{j}_{2}}
. In (
4.50
), we now consider the finite eventually periodic indices
[\mathbf{I},\mathbf{J}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{k(\textsf{p}l+1)}
:

d_{\mathbf{J}}:=T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}+\Sigma_{i=1}^{l}T_{k}^{\textsf{p}(l-i)}d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}=d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}+(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}-I)[(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}+d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}],\quad\quad d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}\in D_{k},\ d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}\in D_{k\textsf{p}}.

d_{\mathbf{I}}:=T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}+\Sigma_{i=1}^{l}T_{k}^{\textsf{p}(l-i)}d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}=d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}+(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}-I)[(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}+d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}],\quad\quad d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\in D_{k},\ d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}\in D_{k\textsf{p}}.

\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}:=J_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}+\Sigma_{i=1}^{l}J_{k}^{\textsf{p}(l-i)}\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}+(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}-I)[(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}+\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}],\quad\quad\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}\in\tilde{A}_{k},\ \tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}\in\tilde{A}_{k\textsf{p}}.

\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}:=J_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}+\Sigma_{i=1}^{l}J_{k}^{\textsf{p}(l-i)}\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}+(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}-I)[(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}+\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}],\quad\quad\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\in\tilde{A}_{k},\ \tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}\in\tilde{A}_{k\textsf{p}}.

Now it is clear that

\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|d_{\mathbf{J}}-d_{\mathbf{I}}|=\infty\Longleftrightarrow\left|(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}(d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|\neq 0

(4.51)

since
J_{k}
in (
1.5
) is expanding and the modulus of its eigenvalues are large when
k
is large.
Similarly,

\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|h(0)|=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}|=\infty\Longleftrightarrow\left|(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})\right|\neq 0.

(4.52)

On the other hand, by Lemma
4.2
-(a),

|h(0)|=|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}|\leq diam(\tilde{F})

(4.53)

for all
l
since
[\mathbf{I},\mathbf{J}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{k(\textsf{p}l+1)}
. Then by (
4.52
), we must have

\left|(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})\right|=0\Longleftrightarrow\left|(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})\right|=0.

(4.54)

By the reverse triangle inequality, this leads to

\frac{|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}|}{||(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}||}\leq\left|(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})\right|\leq\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}\right|\leq diam(\tilde{F})

(4.55)

in view of
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}},\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{k{\textsf{p}}}
.
This is possible if
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|=0
uniformly in
\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}
with

[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{k}
. (Otherwise, it would be bounded away from zero for a subsequence
\{k_{\textsf{n}}\}
and for some
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{k_{\textsf{n}}}
, that is, there exists
c>0
such that

\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|\geq c
for all
k_{\textsf{n}}=|\mathbf{j}_{1}|=|\mathbf{i}_{1}|
. But
||J_{k_{\textsf{n}}}^{\textsf{p}}-I||
grows indefinitely in
n
(or
\underset{{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}}{\lim}||(J_{k_{\textsf{n}}}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}||=0
), because the eigenvalues of
J_{k}
approaches infinity in absolute value. Then (
4.55
) would be wrong.)
But

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|P^{-1}(a_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|=0\Longleftrightarrow\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|a_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|=0.

Since
a_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{1}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}
are integer vectors, there exists a positive integer
k_{\textsf{r}}\geq\textsf{r}
such that
a_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}=a_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
. So for each
[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]\in E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
, there correspond multi-indices
\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}
(of length
k
) satisfying the equality

\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}
.
From this equality,
we actually get the result.
By concatenating
l
such pairs of indices, we have
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}
:

Assuming
d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}\rfloor,\ \ d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\rfloor
as in the proof of the first inclusion
E_{k}(\textsf{r})\subseteq E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
,
we get

0=d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
. By (
4.54
), we have
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}\right|=0
too.
Recall that
[\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{2}]
is a
p
-path in
G_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}
. Using the same type of reasoning and induction, one can show that

0=d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}
for large
k
. For that, one should take into account of the fact that each part of
[\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{2}]
is a path
in
G_{F(J,\tilde{A})}
so that Lemma
4.2
-(a) can be used again.
Thus

\left|(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}(d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})\right|=\left|(d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)(d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})\right|=0

for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
. Then

\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|d_{\mathbf{J}}-d_{\mathbf{I}}|\neq\infty

by (
4.51
). Finally, Lemma
4.2
-(b) leads to

[\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}]\in E_{k}(\textsf{r})
(in fact,
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E_{k}
too).
Therefore,

E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}\subseteq E_{k}(\textsf{r})
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}\geq\textsf{r}.

\blacksquare

We will use eventually periodic indices as in the preceding proof several times in this section.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\tilde{F}=F(J,\tilde{A})
and
F_{k}
be its perturbations.
For any given positive integer
r
, there exists an integer
k_{\textsf{r}}\geq\textsf{r}
such that for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}},
we have

\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})=E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
.

Proof.
Note that we can rephrase this lemma as
“
For large
k
, the tails of edge labels of
G_{k}
coincide with some finite paths of
G_{\tilde{F}}
”.
The inclusion

E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}\subseteq\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})
is obtained as follows:

If
[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
,
then
\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}\neq\emptyset
, that is,
\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}},\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}
are neighboring pieces of
\tilde{F}
. Thus there exist multi-indices

\mathbf{i}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{0}
such that
\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}\neq\emptyset
and
|\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}|=|\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}|=k
is arbitrarily large. For example, one may choose any multi-index
\mathbf{i}_{0}
so that
\mathbf{j}_{0}=\mathbf{i}_{0}
.

Set

\mathbf{i}_{1}=\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{j}_{1}=\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}.

As before, extend
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E^{k}_{\tilde{F}}
(on the right) to eventually periodic
finite paths in
G_{\tilde{F}}
in the form of
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{i}_{3}},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{j}_{3}}]
so that
|\mathbf{i}_{2}|=|\mathbf{j}_{2}|=k
and
|\mathbf{i}_{3}|=|\mathbf{j}_{3}|=k\textsf{p}
as in the proof of Lemma
4.3
.
By Lemma
4.2
-(a)

\ |\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}}|\leq diam(\tilde{F})

for all large
k
. Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.3
that
l
is the number of appearances of the repeating block
\mathbf{i}_{3}
in
\overline{\mathbf{i}_{3}}
. Then setting
\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{i}_{3}},\ \mathbf{J}=\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{j}_{3}}
,
one can again conclude that
\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|d_{\mathbf{J}}-d_{\mathbf{I}}|\neq\infty
for all large
k

since
|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}|\leq diam(\tilde{F})
.
That gives
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E_{k}
by Lemma
4.2
-(b) leading to
E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}\subseteq\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})
.
This argument also works the other way around.

For large k, there are no other types of edge labels of
E_{k}
:
Conversely, consider any pair of multi-indices
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}
with
[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})
. Thus
|\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}|=|\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}|=r
and there exist
a pair
\mathbf{i}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{0}
of multi-indices such that
[\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in E_{k}
(
k\geq r
).
As above, extend
[\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]
(on the right) to eventually periodic
finite paths in
G_{k}
in the form of
[\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}\mathbf{i}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{i}_{3}},\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}\mathbf{j}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{j}_{3}}]
so that
|\mathbf{i}_{2}|=|\mathbf{j}_{2}|=k
and
|\mathbf{i}_{3}|=|\mathbf{j}_{3}|=k\textsf{p}
. Since these paths are in
G_{k}
for each
k
, we have
\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|d_{\mathbf{J}}-d_{\mathbf{I}}|\neq\infty

by Lemma
4.2
-(a). As in the previous proofs, conclude that there is an integer
k_{r}
such that
d_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}=d_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}
,
d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}=d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}
,

d_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}=d_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}
for
k\geq k_{r}.
Then

|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}|,|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}|,|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}|<n
by (
1.4
). Recall that we can choose
P
so that the distance between two distinct lattice points in
P^{-1}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is greater than
n
. This forces
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}
,
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}
,
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}
because
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}},\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}
,
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}},\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}
,
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}},\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}\in\tilde{A}_{k}=P^{-1}A_{k}\subset P^{-1}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
Hence

\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}|<\infty
. By Lemma
4.2
-(b),
[\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in E^{\textsf{k}}_{\tilde{F}}
for all large
k.
This gives
[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
.
That is,
\overline{E}_{k}(\textsf{r})\subseteq E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}
for
k\geq k_{\textsf{r}}
.

\blacksquare

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\tilde{F}=F(J,\tilde{A})
and
(E_{k})^{\textsf{r}}
denote the set of
r
-paths in
E_{k}
. There is an integer
k_{0}
such that

E^{\textsf{r}k}_{\tilde{F}}=(E_{k})^{\textsf{r}}
(
\textsf{r}\in\mathbb{N}
)

for
k\geq k_{0}
.

Proof.
We again use the capital-bold-letter notation in (
4.50
) for multi-index concatenation like

\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{j}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{j}_{\textsf{s}},\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{i}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{i}_{\textsf{s}},\quad\quad|\mathbf{j}_{1}|=\cdots=|\mathbf{j}_{\textsf{s}}|=|\mathbf{i}_{1}|=\cdots=|\mathbf{i}_{\textsf{s}}|=k.

It is enough to demonstrate the proof for
\textsf{r}=1
and
\textsf{r}=2
(the adjacency relation of the edges of
E_{k}
) since the general case follows from these two cases.

(a) Let
\textsf{r}=1
. For the inclusion
E_{k}\subseteq E^{k}_{\tilde{F}}
, let
[\mathbf{i}_{1}(k),\mathbf{j}_{1}(k)]\in E_{k}
.
By Lemma
4.3
and Lemma
4.4
, for large
k
, all edges of
E_{k}
are in the form of
[\mathbf{i}_{1}=\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{1}=\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]
, where
|\mathbf{i}_{1}|=|\mathbf{j}_{1}|=k\geq\textsf{r}
and

[\mathbf{i}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{0}],[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}.

On the other hand, as in the proof of Lemma
4.3
, we can extend
[\mathbf{i}_{1}(k),\mathbf{j}_{1}(k)]
to eventually periodic finite (or infinite) paths
[\mathbf{I}_{1}(k)\overline{\mathbf{I}_{2}(k)},\mathbf{J}_{1}(k)\overline{\mathbf{J}_{2}(k)}]
in
G_{k}
and we get

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|d_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}\right|=0.

This eventually leads to
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|=0
(see the arguments in the proof of Lemma
4.3
). Since
d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}
are integer vectors, there exists a positive integer
k_{0}
such that
d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}(k)=d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}(k)
for
k\geq k_{0}
.
In view of
d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}\rfloor,\ \ d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\rfloor
, that is possible only if
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}
, as a function of
k
,
is bounded. Now we rewrite
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}
as

\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}=J_{k}^{\textsf{r}}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}})+\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}.

For this to be bounded, we must have

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|P^{-1}(a_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}-a_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}})\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}}\right|=0

because
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}\right|\leq diam(\tilde{F})
by Lemma
4.2
-(a).
This gives
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}=\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}}
for large
k
because

a_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}}
are integer vectors (or
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}},\ \tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}}
are lattice points).
We then conclude that

\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\cap\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}=(\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}})_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}\cap(\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}})_{\mathbf{j}_{0}\mathbf{j}}=(\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}})_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}\cap(\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}})_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}=(\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}})_{\mathbf{i}_{0}\mathbf{i}}\neq\emptyset

for all large
k

because
[\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}]\in E^{\textsf{r}}_{\tilde{F}}\Longrightarrow\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}_{0}}\cap\tilde{F}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}_{0}}\neq\emptyset
.
That leads to
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E^{k}_{\tilde{F}}
.

The above argument works in the other direction either. That is,

E^{k}_{\tilde{F}}\subseteq E_{k}
for large
k
. As a result, there is an integer
k_{0}
such that
E^{k}_{\tilde{F}}=E_{k}
for
k\geq k_{0}
.

(b) Let
\textsf{r}=2
. Next we can come to the issue of the adjacency relation of the edges of
E_{k}
. If
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{2k},
then we use the idea in (a)
to show that
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in(E_{k})^{2}
. The difference is we work with
2
-paths in
G_{k}
rather than edges.

By using the neighbor graph
G_{\tilde{F}}
of the original fractal
\tilde{F}
and sufficiently large
k
, every
2k
-path
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{2k}
can be extended (on the right) to eventually periodic
finite paths in the form of
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}\mathbf{i}_{3}\overline{\mathbf{i}_{4}},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}\mathbf{j}_{3}\overline{\mathbf{j}_{4}}]
so that
|\mathbf{i}_{3}|=|\mathbf{j}_{3}|=k
and
|\mathbf{i}_{4}|=|\mathbf{j}_{4}|=k\textsf{p}
.

We now consider the finite eventually periodic indices
\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}\mathbf{i}_{3}\overline{\mathbf{i}_{4}}
,
\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}\mathbf{j}_{3}\overline{\mathbf{j}_{4}}
. Let
l
be the number of appearances of the repeating multi-index
\mathbf{j}_{4}
in the block
\overline{\mathbf{j}_{4}}
. Then

d_{\mathbf{J}}:=T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}\mathbf{j}_{3}}+\Sigma_{i=1}^{l}T_{k}^{\textsf{p}(l-i)}d_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}=T_{k}^{2}d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}+T_{k}d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}+d_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}+(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}-I)[(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}d_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}+T_{k}^{2}d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}+T_{k}d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}+d_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}],

d_{\mathbf{I}}:=T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}\mathbf{i}_{3}}+\Sigma_{i=1}^{l}T_{k}^{\textsf{p}(l-i)}d_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}=T_{k}^{2}d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}+T_{k}d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}+d_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}+(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}-I)[(T_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}d_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}+T_{k}^{2}d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}+T_{k}d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}+d_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}],

where
d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}},d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}},d_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}\in D_{k},\ d_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}\in D_{k\textsf{p}},\quad d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}},d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}},d_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}\in D_{k},\ d_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}\in D_{k\textsf{p}}
.
To define
\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}},\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}
, we simply replace
d
by
\tilde{a}
and
T
by
J
above. Since the rest of the argument is as in (a),
we give a sketch of it.
Lemma
4.2
-(a) gives

|h(0)|=|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}|\leq diam(\tilde{F})

for all
l
since
[\mathbf{I},\mathbf{J}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{k(\textsf{p}l+3)}
.
Since
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}\mathbf{i}_{3},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}\mathbf{j}_{3}]\in E_{\tilde{F}}^{3k},
we also have

|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}\mathbf{i}_{3}}|=\left|J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})\right|\leq diam(\tilde{F}).

By the reverse triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\tiny{\left|(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}l}-I)\left[(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{4}})+J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})\right]\right|}\leq

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}\right|+\left|J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})\right|\leq 2diam(\tilde{F}).

In order for this to be true for all
l
, we must have

\left[(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{4}})+J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})\right]=0.\\

But

\left[(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)^{-1}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{4}})+J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})\right]=0\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\left|(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{4}})+(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)[J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})]\right|=0.

(4.56)

By the reverse triangle inequality again, this leads to

\left|(J_{k}^{\textsf{p}}-I)\left[J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})\right]\right|\leq\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}\right|\leq diam(\tilde{F})

This is possible if
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}})\right|=0
as in part (a). Thus

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}\right|=0

by (
4.56
), and similarly

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|J_{k}^{2}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})\right|\leq\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}\right|\leq diam(\tilde{F}).

Again that is possible
if
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|J_{k}(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}})+(\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}})\right|=0.
Then

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}\right|=0
by (
4.56
) again.
Continuing in this way, we get

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}\right|=0.

Then
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|a_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|a_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|a_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}\right|=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left|a_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}\right|=0.

Due to the fact that
a_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{1}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{2}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{3}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-a_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}
are integer vectors, there exists a positive integer
k_{0}
such that
a_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}=a_{\mathbf{i}_{1}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}=a_{\mathbf{i}_{2}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}=a_{\mathbf{i}_{3}},\ a_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}=a_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}
for
k\geq k_{0}
.
Thus in view of
d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}\rfloor,\ \ d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}=\lfloor\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\rfloor
,
we have

d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}=0
for
k\geq k_{0}
. Similarly,
d_{\mathbf{j}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{2}}=0,\ d_{\mathbf{j}_{3}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{3}}=0,\ d_{\mathbf{j}_{4}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{4}}=0
for
k\geq k_{0}
,
and hence

\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}|d_{\mathbf{J}}-d_{\mathbf{I}})|\neq\infty.

Finally, part (a) and Lemma
4.2
-(b) yield

[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in(E_{k})^{2}
.
Therefore,
E^{2k}_{\tilde{F}}\subseteq(E_{k})^{2}
for
k\geq k_{0}.

The other inclusion
(E_{k})^{2}\subseteq E^{2k}_{\tilde{F}}
is handled analogously.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.6
.

Proposition
4.5
shows that not only are the dimensions of
\tilde{F}
and its perturbations
F_{k}
related, but also their topologies. This is because the topological properties (like simple connectedness, the disk-like or the ball-like property
[
3
, Theorem 9]
) of
\tilde{F}
are usually given in terms of its graph. For instance,

\tilde{F}
is connected if and only if
F_{k}
is connected for all large
k
. The proof directly follows from
[
23
, Theorem 4.3]
and Proposition
4.5
.
\Box

Further, the following is an immediate consequence of Proposition
4.5
and its proof. It implies that the points or subsets of
\tilde{F}=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
and
F_{k}
given by the correspondence (
2.10
)
eventually (i.e. for large
k
) have the same multiple codings (index sequences).

A
graph homomorphism
from a graph
G=(V_{G},E_{G})
to a graph
H=(V_{H},E_{H})
is a function from the vertex set
V_{G}
to the vertex set
V_{H}
which is
edge-preserving
[
16
]
. A
graph isomorphism
of graphs
G
and
H
is a bijection between the vertex sets of
G
and
H
which is both edge-preserving and label-preserving

[
13
, p.424]
.

Corollary 4.7
.

There exists an integer
k_{0}
such that there is a label-preserving homomorphism from
G_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}
onto
G_{k}
for each
k\geq k_{0}
.
In particular, the sets of infinite paths of
G_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}
and
G_{k}
are the same for all large
k
.

Proof.
Now a graph homomorphism is apparent:

By Proposition
4.5
, when
\tilde{F}=F(J,\tilde{A})
we have
E^{\textsf{r}k}_{\tilde{F}}=(E_{k})^{\textsf{r}}
for
k\geq k_{0}
. Moreover, from the proof of the same proposition we know that
[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E^{k}_{\tilde{F}}\Longleftrightarrow[\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{j}_{1}]\in E_{k},\quad\quad[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in E^{2k}_{\tilde{F}}\Longleftrightarrow[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in(E_{k})^{2}
for
k\geq k_{0}
.
Then we can define an edge-preserving and label-preserving homomorphism
by the vertex function

\varphi_{k}:V_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}\rightarrow V_{k}
with
h(x)=x+\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\underset{\varphi_{k}}{\longmapsto}h_{k}(x)=x+d_{\mathbf{j_{1}}}-d_{\mathbf{i_{1}}}

where
[\mathbf{i_{1}},\mathbf{j_{1}}]\in E_{k}
. Then the edges for
\varphi_{k}\left(V_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}\right)
will be as follows.
When
[\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}]\in(E_{k})^{2}
, we let

x+d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}}\underset{[\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{j}_{2}]}{\longrightarrow}x+d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}}.

In this case, we naturally define
\varphi_{k}\left(x+\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}}\right)=x+d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}}
.
In general, if
\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{i}_{1}\mathbf{i}_{2}...\mathbf{i}_{\textsf{r}},\ \ \mathbf{J}=\mathbf{j}_{1}\mathbf{j}_{2}...\mathbf{j}_{\textsf{r}}
and

[\mathbf{I},\mathbf{J}]\in E^{\textsf{r}k}_{\tilde{F}}
, then

\varphi_{k}\left(x+\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{J}}-\tilde{a}_{\mathbf{I}}\right)=x+d_{\mathbf{J}}-d_{\mathbf{I}}

and

x+d_{\mathbf{j}_{1}...\mathbf{j}_{\textsf{r-1}}}-d_{\mathbf{i}_{1}...\mathbf{i}_{\textsf{r-1}}}\underset{[\mathbf{i}_{r},\mathbf{j}_{r}]}{\longrightarrow}x+d_{\mathbf{J}}-d_{\mathbf{I}}.

So the edge-label set for
\varphi_{k}\left(V_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}\right)
is the same as
E^{k}_{\tilde{F}}
, i.e.
\varphi_{k}
is label-preserving. But
\varphi_{k}\left(V_{F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})}\right)=V_{k}
by Proposition
4.5
again.
Then
\varphi_{k}
is a surjective homomorphism for
k\geq k_{0}
.

\blacksquare

Corollary
4.7
is sufficient for our purposes. In fact,
\varphi_{k}
is a graph isomorphism for
k\geq k_{0}
due to our special choice of
P
in the introduction section:

If
x+d_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}=\varphi_{k}\left(x+\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}}\right)=\varphi_{k}\left(x+\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{2}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}}\right)=x+d_{\mathbf{J}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}
, then
d_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}=d_{\mathbf{J}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}
.
Recall that we can choose
P
so that the distance between two distinct lattice points in
P^{-1}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is greater than
n
(or any number as we wish).
This forces
\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}}=\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{2}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}}
for
k\geq k_{0}
,
as we observed in the last paragraph of the proof of Lemma
4.4
. In a similar fashion, one can show that
\varphi_{k}
is a well-defined for
k\geq k_{0}
, i.e. if
x+\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}}=x+\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{2}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}}
for possibly different paths
[\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{J}_{1}],\ [\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{J}_{2}]
, then
\varphi_{k}\left(x+\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}}\right)=x+d_{\mathbf{J}_{1}}-d_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}=x+d_{\mathbf{J}_{2}}-d_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}=\varphi_{k}\left(x+\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{J}_{2}}}-\tilde{a}_{{}_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}}\right)
.

5
An Application:
The Box Dimension

From now on, we may tacitly work with sufficiently large indices
k
so that the results in the previous sections apply.

Lemma 5.1
.

(cf. Proposition 3.1 in
[
20
]
)
Let
F=F(T,A)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a self-affine set.
Then there exists an
integral self-affine tile
{\Gamma}

such that
F\subset{\Gamma}
.

Proof.
Let
m\geq 2
be an integer and
I\in M_{n}({\mathbb{Z}})
denote the identity matrix. Simply take
M=mI
and

D^{\prime}=\{{\tiny(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots i_{n})\ :\ i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{n}\in\{0,1,...,m-1\}}\}

so that

F(M,D^{\prime})
is the unit n-cube. Then it is easy to see that there exist
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
c_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\Gamma:=F(M,D)
has the required property
for
D=a+c_{0}D^{\prime}
.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.2
.

The condition
F\subset{\Gamma}
is also needed to represent an integral self-affine set as a graph-directed set for dimension computations. In that case,
we may use an auxiliary tile
{\Gamma}
(see
[
15
]
or Appendix), which is not necessarily a cube.

\hfill\Box

Now we start with our box-dimension consideration. Let
\lambda=|\lambda_{1}|
be the minimum of the absolute values of the eigenvalues of
J
(the Jordan form) and set
m=\lfloor\lambda\rfloor
. Note that
\lambda^{k}
is
r_{1}
in Definition
3.1
. Here we are assuming that
2\leq m
. Take
M=mI
(
I
is the identity matrix) as the
generating matrix of the tile
\Gamma
containing
\tilde{F},F_{k}.

Let
\Gamma=F(M,D)
be the cube in
the proof of Lemma
5.1
and
N
be the number of elements in
D
.
We next define the index set
\Sigma_{r}=\{\textbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{r}):1\leq i_{j}\leq N\}.

For

\textbf{i}\in\Sigma_{r}
, we set

d_{\textbf{i}}=\Sigma_{j=1}^{r}M^{(k-j)}d_{i_{j}}
, where
d_{i_{j}}\in D
.
Let

\psi_{\textbf{i}}(\Gamma)=M^{-r}(\Gamma+d_{\textbf{i}}).

Then we can cover
\tilde{F},F_{k}
by the smaller n-cubes

\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}:=\psi_{\textbf{i}}(\Gamma)\subseteq\Gamma

(5.57)

of side lengths
c_{0}m^{-r}
, where
r
is an arbitrarily fixed positive integer.
These cubes are also known as
mesh cubes
.

When
k\neq 1
, it seems more appropriate to use the longer, but more precise notation
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
for the pieces of
\tilde{F}=(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})=P^{-1}F
, see Remark
3.2
-(b).
First, we would like to express the box dimension formula in terms of pieces
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
and related sets to be defined.

Perhaps we should also mention the following, which indicates that no distinct-level pieces
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
,
P_{l^{\prime},k}(\tilde{F})
with
l^{\prime}\geq 2l
can be commensurable with the same size cubes for large
k.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\tilde{F}=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
. If
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
is commensurable with an
n
-cube of side length
c_{0}m^{-r}
, then
P_{l^{\prime},k}(\tilde{F})
is not so
for
l^{\prime}\geq 2l
and large
k,l.
In fact,

diam(P_{l^{\prime},k}(\tilde{F}))<diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))

for
l^{\prime}\geq 2l
and all large
k,l
. The same is true for
P_{l}(F_{k})
or related sets
S_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
,
S_{l}(F_{k})
to be defined.

Proof.

It suffices to work with the harder case of
m_{p},l_{p}.

Let
\iota:=\iota_{1}
be a positive integer. Note that

l^{\prime}_{1}:=l^{\prime}\geq l+\iota=l_{1}+\iota_{1}\Longrightarrow l^{\prime}_{p}:=(l^{\prime})_{p}\geq l_{p}+\iota_{p}

(5.58)

by an induction argument: Assume that
l^{\prime}_{p}:=(l^{\prime})_{p}\geq l_{p}+\iota_{p}
(
1\leq p\leq s-1
). Then

\displaystyle l^{\prime}_{p+1}=\left\lfloor l^{\prime}_{p}\frac{\log{m_{p}}}{\log{m_{p+1}}}\right\rfloor

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left\lfloor(l_{p}+\iota_{p})\frac{\log{m_{p}}}{\log{m_{p+1}}}\right\rfloor\ \hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\mathrm{by\ the\ definition\ of}\ l^{\prime}_{p+1}\ \mathrm{and\ the\ induction\ hypothesis}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left\lfloor l_{p}\frac{\log{m_{p}}}{\log{m_{p+1}}}\right\rfloor+\left\lfloor\iota_{p}\frac{\log{m_{p}}}{\log{m_{p+1}}}\right\rfloor\ \hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\mathrm{by\ a\ property\ of\ the\ floor\ function}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle l_{p+1}+\iota_{p+1}\ \ \hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\ \ \mathrm{by\ the\ definitions\ of}\ l_{p+1},\ \iota_{p+1}

which finishes the induction argument.

Next we justify the inequality
diam(P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k}))\leq c_{0}m^{-r-1}
for
l^{\prime}\geq 2l
and large
k
:
We have

(P_{l}(F_{k}))_{p}\subseteq\sum_{i=1}^{{l}_{p}}T_{k,p}^{-i}(d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}+T_{k,p}^{-{l}_{p}}(F_{k})_{p},\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt(P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k}))_{p}\subseteq\sum_{i=1}^{{l}^{\prime}_{p}}T_{k,p}^{-i}(d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}})_{p}+T_{k,p}^{-{l}^{\prime}_{p}}(F_{k})_{p}\ \ \ \ \mathrm{(when}\ l\ \mathrm{is\ replaced\ by}\ l^{\prime})

by Definition
3.1
-(ii).
Hence, for
l^{\prime}\geq 2l
, (
5.58
) yields

\displaystyle diam\left((P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k}))_{p}\right)\leq diam\left(T_{k,p}^{-{l}^{\prime}_{p}}(F_{k})_{p}\right)\leq||T_{k,p}^{-l_{p}}||\ diam\left(T_{k,p}^{-l_{p}}(F_{k})_{p}\right)

so that

\footnotesize diam(P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k}))\leq\sum_{p=1}^{s}diam\left((P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k}))_{p}\right)\leq\sum_{p=1}^{s}||T_{k,p}^{-l_{p}}||\ diam\left(T_{k,p}^{-l_{p}}(F_{k})_{p}\right)\leq||T_{k,p}^{-l_{s}}||\ diam\left(F_{k}\right)\sum_{p=1}^{s}||T_{k,p}^{-l_{p}}||

since
l_{s}\leq l_{p}
.
As in (
3.41
), for large
k
, we get

\displaystyle diam(P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||T_{k,p}^{-l_{s}}||\ diam\left(F_{k}\right)\sum_{p=1}^{s}||T_{k,p}^{-l_{p}}||\leq||T_{k,p}^{-l_{s}}||\ \overset{s}{\underset{p=1}{\Sigma}}c^{\prime\prime}l_{p}^{\alpha_{0}}(m_{p})^{-l_{p}}

(5.59)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||T_{k,p}^{-l_{s}}||\ \sum_{p=1}^{s}c^{\prime\prime}l^{\alpha_{0}}(m_{1})^{-l}\leq||T_{k,p}^{-l_{s}}||\ c^{\prime\prime}l^{\alpha_{0}}(|\lambda_{1}|^{k})^{-l}

since
m_{p}\geq m_{1}=\lceil r_{1}\rceil\geq r_{1}=|\lambda_{1}|^{k}.

Assume that
2\leq m:=\lfloor|\lambda_{1}|\rfloor
.
As in the proof of Lemma
2.7
, choose a constant
\eta
such that
1<\eta<m\leq|\lambda_{1}|\leq|\lambda_{p}|
. Then there is an integer
k_{0}
such that

||T_{k,p}^{-l_{s}}||\leq\frac{c^{\prime}}{\eta^{kl_{s}}}<\frac{1}{\eta l_{s}}
for
k>k_{0}
. But there is an integer
l_{0}
such that
\frac{1}{\eta l_{s}}\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{c^{\prime}}l^{2\alpha_{0}}<\frac{1}{m^{2}}

for
l>l_{0}
. Because,
l_{s}\approx l\frac{\log m_{1}}{\log m_{s}}
by (
3.40
) (with
|\lambda_{1}|,|\lambda_{s}|
replaced by
m_{1},m_{s}
) and

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log m_{1}}{\log m_{s}}=\frac{\log|\lambda_{1}|}{\log|\lambda_{s}|}
(thus,
\textsf{c}_{1}l\leq l_{s}\leq\textsf{c}_{2}l
for some constants
\textsf{c}_{1},\textsf{c}_{2}
, or roughly
l_{s}\approx l
).
Thus

\displaystyle diam\left(P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta l_{s}}c^{\prime\prime}l^{\alpha_{0}}(|\lambda_{1}|^{k})^{-l}\leq\frac{1}{\eta l_{s}}\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{c^{\prime}}l^{2\alpha_{0}}diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\hskip 8.50012pt\ \ \mathrm{by}\ (\ref{Gen4}),\ (\ref{piece_cylinder})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\leq\frac{1}{m^{2}}c_{0}m^{-r+1}=c_{0}m^{-r-1}\ \hskip 8.50012pt\mathrm{by\ the\ commensurability\ of}\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\

for
k>k_{0}
and
l>l_{0}
. That is,
diam(P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k})),\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))
are not commensurable with the same cube.
These inequalities also show that
diam(P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{k}))<diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))
at the same time.

\blacksquare

One can
always maintain
the property in the above lemma by replacing the initial generating data
J_{1},\tilde{A}_{1}
for
\tilde{F}
by
J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k}
for some large
k.

We now remember a couple of numbers obtained as a result of box (or piece) counting process, in which
\tilde{F}
may be replaced by
F_{k}
as usual.

•

N^{r}(\tilde{F})
denotes the number of mesh cubes
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
of
\Gamma
(with side length
c_{0}m^{-r}
) that intersect
\tilde{F}
.

•

In view of Lemma
5.3
, consider the levels
l\leq\ l^{\prime}\leq 2l
. Let
N_{l}(\tilde{F})
be the number of all pieces

P_{l^{\prime},k}(\tilde{F})
of
\tilde{F}
, which intersect and are commensurable with an
n
-cube
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
in the previous item.

Lemma
5.3
takes care of the question whether the number of the pieces that intersect and are commensurable with an
n
-cube
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
cripples the proofs. In the proofs, considering the number of all pieces
P_{l^{\prime},k}(\tilde{F})
in the last item rather than the number of all pieces

P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
will not affect our proofs negatively. That is why, we may ignore
l^{\prime}
and just mention the level-
l
pieces
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
sometimes.

Another question that arises is whether there is a piece
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
or
P_{l}(F_{k})
which intersect and is commensurable with a given
n
-cube
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
as in the first item. To answer the question, we will cover
F_{k}
by mesh
n
-cubes
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
with side length
c_{0}(m^{r_{k}})^{-r}
(rather than
c_{0}m^{-r}
) for some integer
r_{k}
. Then we use the fact that a larger piece intersects every such small cube and that piece is a finite union of smaller pieces.
We also need to study the diameters
more closely.

Lemma 5.4
.

There is a positive integer
r_{k}
such that given
n
-cube
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
with side length
c_{0}m^{-r_{k}r}
, there is a piece which intersect and is commensurable with
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
.

Proof.

In Definition
3.1
,
we have mentioned that every piece is between two (cylinder) sets in
F_{k}
. That is, if we set
\mathbf{I}_{1}=\mathbf{j}_{1}...\mathbf{j}_{l}
(with
l=l_{1}=\tilde{l}_{1}
) and
\mathbf{I}_{s}=\mathbf{j}_{1}...\mathbf{j}_{l_{s}}
, then

\displaystyle(F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{l_{1}}T_{k}^{-i}d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}+T_{k}^{-l_{1}}F_{k}\subseteq P_{l}(F_{k})\subseteq\sum_{i=1}^{l_{s}}T_{k}^{-i}d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}+T_{k}^{-l_{s}}F_{k}=(F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{s}},

(5.60)

\displaystyle(\tilde{F})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{1}}J_{k}^{-i}d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}+J_{k}^{-\tilde{l}_{1}}\tilde{F}\subseteq P_{l}(\tilde{F})\subseteq\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}_{s}}J_{k}^{-i}d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}+J_{k}^{-\tilde{l}_{s}}\tilde{F}=(\tilde{F})_{\mathbf{I}_{s}},

and
P_{l}(F_{k})
(or
P_{l}(\tilde{F})
) is a finite union of the cylinder sets
(F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}

for certain
d_{{\mathbf{j}_{i}}}\in D_{k}
(
1\leq i\leq l_{1}=l
), but
d_{{\mathbf{j}_{1}}},...,d_{{\mathbf{j}_{l_{s}}}}
are fixed. It is adequate to give the proof for
F_{k}
.
Then

\displaystyle diam(T_{k}^{-l_{1}}F_{k})=diam((F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}})\leq diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\leq diam((F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{s}})=diam(T_{k}^{-l_{s}}F_{k})

(5.61)

by (
5.60
).

(a)

diam(T_{k}^{-l_{1}}F_{k})\approx diam(T_{k}^{-l_{s}}F_{k})\approx diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))
:

We estimate the diameters

diam((F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{1}})=diam(T_{k}^{-l_{1}}F_{k}),\quad\quad\quad diam((F_{k})_{\mathbf{I}_{s}})=diam(T_{k}^{-l_{s}}F_{k}).

For that, we estimate the norms. Recall
T_{k,p}

in (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) :

T_{k,p}=\lceil C_{p}^{k}\rceil\odot\left(I+N_{2}\right)\quad\quad\mathrm{so\ that}\quad\quad T_{k,p}^{-l_{p}}=\lceil C_{p}^{k}\rceil^{-l_{p}}\odot\left(I+N_{2}\right)^{-l_{p}}.

Now

l_{p}\approx l\frac{\log|\lambda_{1}|}{\log|\lambda_{p}|}
.
Because
l_{p}\approx l\frac{\log m_{1}}{\log m_{p}}
by (
3.40
) (with
|\lambda_{1}|,|\lambda_{p}|
replaced by
m_{1},m_{p}
) and

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log m_{1}}{\log m_{p}}=\frac{\log|\lambda_{1}|}{\log|\lambda_{p}|}
.
It follows that

||T_{k}^{-l_{1}}x||\approx||T_{k}^{-l_{s}}x||
,
where
x\in R^{n}
.
Hence for every positive integer
l
,

\displaystyle diam(T_{k}^{-l_{1}}F_{k})\approx diam(T_{k}^{-l_{s}}F_{k})\Longrightarrow diam(T_{k}^{-l_{1}}F_{k})\approx diam(T_{k}^{-l_{s}}F_{k})\approx diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))

by (
5.61
).

(b)

\frac{\textsf{c}^{\prime}}{||T_{k}||}\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\ \leq diam(P_{l+1}(F_{k}))\leq\textsf{c}^{\prime\prime}||T_{k}^{-1}||\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))
:

By the previous item,
diam(P_{l+1}(F_{k}))\approx diam(T_{k}^{-(l+1)}F_{k})
so that

\frac{\textsf{c}^{\prime}}{||T_{k}||}\ diam(T_{k}^{-l}F_{k})\leq\textsf{c}^{\prime}diam(T_{k}^{-(l+1)}F_{k})\leq diam(P_{l+1}(F_{k}))\leq\textsf{c}^{\prime\prime}diam(T_{k}^{-(l+1)}F_{k})\leq\textsf{c}^{\prime\prime}||T_{k}^{-1}||\ diam(T_{k}^{-l}F_{k}).

for some positive constants
\textsf{c}^{\prime},\textsf{c}^{\prime\prime}
. Again by (a),
diam(T_{k}^{-l}F_{k})\approx diam(P_{l}(F_{k})).
This gives

\displaystyle\frac{\textsf{c}^{\prime}}{||T_{k}||}\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\leq diam(P_{l+1}(F_{k}))\leq\textsf{c}^{\prime\prime}||T_{k}^{-1}||\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k})).

by modification of
\textsf{c}^{\prime},\textsf{c}^{\prime\prime}
.

(c)

Choose a positive integer
r_{k}
such that
1\leq\frac{\textsf{c}^{\prime}}{||T_{k}||}m^{r_{k}}
, where
c^{\prime}
is as in (b). We cover
F_{k}
by mesh
n
-cubes
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
with side length
c_{0}m^{-r_{k}r}
. Now given a small mesh
n
-cube
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
that intersect
F_{k}
,
there exist a level
l
and pieces
P_{l}(F_{k}),\ P_{l+1}(F_{k})
such that they intersect
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
and

diam(P_{l+1}(F_{k}))\leq c_{0}(m^{r_{k}})^{-r+1}<diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))

because
P_{l}(F_{k})
is a finite union of smaller pieces
P_{l+1}(F_{k})
.
Notice that
c_{0}(m^{r_{k}})^{-r+1}<diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))
implies that
P_{l}(F_{k})
is not commensurable with
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
.
Then

c_{0}(m^{r_{k}})^{-r}\leq\frac{\textsf{c}^{\prime}}{||T_{k}||}c_{0}(m^{r_{k}})^{-r+1}<\frac{\textsf{c}^{\prime}}{||T_{k}||}\ diam(P_{l}(F_{k}))\leq diam(P_{l+1}(F_{k}))

by (b). Consequently,
c_{0}(m^{r_{k}})^{-r}<diam(P_{l+1}(F_{k}))\leq c_{0}(m^{r_{k}})^{-r+1}
, i.e.
P_{l+1}(F_{k})

is commensurable with
\textsf{C}_{\textbf{i}}
.

\blacksquare

By definition, the
lower box dimension
and the
upper box dimension
of
\tilde{F}
, denoted by
\underline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}
and
\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}
, are given by

\displaystyle\underline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\liminf}\frac{\log N^{r}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{\log N^{r}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}},

\displaystyle\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\limsup}\frac{\log N^{r}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{\log N^{r}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}.

When
\underline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}
, this common value is denoted by
{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}
, called the
box dimension
of
\tilde{F}
, and we say that the box dimension exists.
We will see that
N^{r}(\tilde{F})
in these formulas can be replaced by
N_{l}(\tilde{F}).

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
F=F(T,A)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an integral self-affine set and let
\tilde{F}=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
. Then

\underline{dim}_{B}F=\underline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))},\quad\ \ \ \overline{dim}_{B}F=\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))}.

\tilde{F}
in these equalities may be replaced especially by
F_{k}
, and
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
by
P_{l}(F_{k})
.

Proof.
In view of Lemma
5.4
, we may assume that for every
n
-cube intersecting
\tilde{F}
, there is a piece
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
commensurable with and intersects that cube. That gives
N^{r}(\tilde{F})\leq N_{l}(\tilde{F})
.
Given a piece
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})
commensurable with an
n
-cube, Lemma
5.3
implies that the number of levels
l^{\prime}
such that a piece
P_{l^{\prime},k}(\tilde{F})
may be commensurable with an
n
-cube is bounded by
l
. For every level
l^{\prime}
, the number of
pieces that intersect and commensurable with that cube is bounded by
U_{l^{\prime}}=\alpha_{1}(l^{\prime})^{\alpha_{2}}
by Lemma
3.5
. We write
l_{i}^{\prime}=l+i-1
,
i=1,2,...,l
, for such possible levels.
Reset
U_{r}:=l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{l}U_{l^{\prime}_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{1}(l+i-1)^{\alpha_{2}}
. For an integral self-affine set
or more exactly, for
\tilde{F}=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
, we thus have

\frac{N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}}}=\frac{N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{U_{r}}\leq N^{r}(\tilde{F})\leq N_{l}(\tilde{F})

(5.62)

for some positive integer constants
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
.
Writing
|\lambda_{1}|=\lambda
in Lemma
3.4
, we then obtain the following estimate

c^{\prime}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{-l}\leq diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))\leq c^{\prime\prime}{l}^{\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{-l}

(5.63)

for suitable constants
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{0}.

These inequalities, together with the commensurability inequalities
c_{1}m^{-r}<diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))\leq c_{2}m^{-r}
,
imply

c_{1}m^{-r}<diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))\leq c^{\prime\prime}{l}^{\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{-l},\quad\quad\quad c^{\prime}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{-l}\leq diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))\leq c_{2}m^{-r}.

That gives

c^{\prime\prime\prime}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{l}<m^{r}\leq c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\ {l}^{\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{l}

(5.64)

(
c^{\prime\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
are constants). Clearly,

l\rightarrow\infty\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ r\rightarrow\infty.

So (
5.64
) leads to

\displaystyle 0=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log(l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}})}{\log c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\ {2l}^{\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{l}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log(l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}})}{\log c^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\ {l}^{\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{l}}\leq\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log(l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}})}{\log m^{r}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log(l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}})}{\log c^{\prime\prime\prime}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{l}}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log(l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}})}{\log c^{\prime\prime\prime}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(\lambda^{k})^{l}}=0.

by standard calculus techniques for limits (like L’H
\hat{\mathrm{o}}
pital’s rule).
This gives

\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log U_{r}}{\log m^{r}}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log(l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}})}{\log m^{r}}=0.

Then by (
5.62
), we get

\frac{\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}-\frac{\log(l\alpha_{1}(2l)^{\alpha_{2}})}{\log m^{r}}=\frac{\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}-\frac{\log U_{r}}{\log m^{r}}\leq\frac{\log N^{r}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}\leq\frac{\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}

(5.65)

so that

\underline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{\log N^{r}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}},\ \ \ \ \overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{\log N^{r}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}=\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log m^{r}}

by the properties of lower and upper limits.
Since we are counting the pieces
P_{l,k}(\tilde{F})

commensurable with an
n
-cube of side length
c_{0}m^{-r}
, we have
c_{1}m^{-r}<diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))\leq c_{2}m^{-r}
. As observed above,

l\rightarrow\infty
if and only if
r\rightarrow\infty
.
Then, we can further write

\underline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))},\quad\ \ \ \overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(P_{l,k}(\tilde{F}))}.

\blacksquare

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
F=F(T,A)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an integral self-affine set and let
\tilde{F}=F(J_{k},\tilde{A}_{k})
.
Then

\overline{dim}_{B}F=\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}\leq\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}{dim}_{B}F_{k}.

Proof.

From real analysis, we know that there is a subsequence
\{F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}\}
of
F_{k}
such that

\displaystyle\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}dim_{B}F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}dim_{B}F_{k}.

(5.66)

Since
dim_{B}F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}
exists by Proposition
9.5
in the Appendix, we can replace it by
\overline{dim}_{B}F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}
in (
5.66
). By Lemma
5.5
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})}{\log diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))}=\overline{dim}_{B}F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}

(5.67)

for every
n
. If we let
\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty
in (
5.67
), then (
5.66
) takes the form of

\displaystyle\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\ \frac{-\log N_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})}{\log diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))}=\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\ \overline{dim}_{B}F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}dim_{B}F_{k}.

(5.68)

Associated with
P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})
, we define the set

S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}):=\{x\in\tilde{F}=F(J_{k_{\textsf{n}}},\tilde{A}_{k_{\textsf{n}}}):\ x_{k_{\textsf{n}}}\in P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})\}\subseteq\tilde{F},

where
x
is related to
x_{k_{\textsf{n}}}
by (
2.10
). Notice that the definition of
S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F})
uses
l_{p}
coming from the definition of
P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})
while that of
P_{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}(\tilde{F})
uses
\tilde{l}_{p}
. Since
l_{p}
and
\tilde{l}_{p}
in Definition
3.1
may be unequal for
p>1
(even if
l_{1}=\tilde{l}_{1}=l
), the sets
S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F})
and
P_{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}(\tilde{F})
may be different. Although
S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F})
is not necessarily the level-
l
piece
P_{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}(\tilde{F})
, we may say that it is approximately a level-
l
piece by Lemma
3.3
. Let
N_{l}^{S}(\tilde{F})
be the number of the sets
S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F})

corresponding to
P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})
. By Proposition
4.5
or Corollary
4.7
, (for large
n
)
N_{l}^{S}(\tilde{F})
is equal to the number
N_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})
of the pieces
P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})
commensurable and intersecting a cube of side length
c_{0}m^{-r}
in a cover of
F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}
by cubes. For the same reason, one may also assume that
S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F})
includes all points
x
obtained from all different series expansions of
x_{k_{\textsf{n}}}
.
In this manner, for each large
n
, we make

\{S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}):l\in\mathbb{N}\}
a cover of
\tilde{F}=F(J_{k_{\textsf{n}}},\tilde{A}_{k_{\textsf{n}}})
.

Let
u_{\textsf{n},l}=\frac{-\log N_{l}^{S}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))}
,
v_{\textsf{n},l}=\frac{\log diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))}{\log diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))}=\frac{-\log diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))}{-\log diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))}.
Then
u_{\textsf{n},l},v_{\textsf{n},l}\geq 0
for all sufficiently large
n
because
diam((S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F})),\ \ diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))<1
for large
n
. That allows us to use the formula

\displaystyle\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}(u_{\textsf{n},l}v_{\textsf{n},l})=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\ u_{\textsf{n},l}\ \lim\ v_{\textsf{n},l}

below when
u_{\textsf{n},l},v_{\textsf{n},l}\geq 0
,
\lim v_{\textsf{n},l}
exists and
\lim v_{\textsf{n},l}>0
.
Then we can write (
5.67
) as

\displaystyle\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\ u_{\textsf{n},l}\ \lim\ v_{\textsf{n},l}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}(u_{\textsf{n},l}v_{\textsf{n},l})=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}^{S}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))}=\overline{dim}_{B}F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}

(5.69)

for large
n
because
N_{l}^{S}(\tilde{F})=N_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}})
.
We now study the limiting behaviour of
v_{\textsf{n},l}
. In particular, we show that both
\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}v_{\textsf{n},l}
and

\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}v_{\textsf{n},l}
exist.
Similar to (
5.63
), from Lemma
2.5
, Remark
2.6
and Lemma
3.4
, we infer that

\displaystyle c^{\prime\prime\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{-c^{\prime\prime}}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(r_{1})^{-l}\leq diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))\leq c^{\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{c^{\prime\prime}}{l}^{\alpha_{0}}(r_{1})^{-l},\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptc^{\prime\prime\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{-c^{\prime\prime}}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(m_{1})^{-l}\leq diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))\leq c^{\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{c^{\prime\prime}}{l}^{\alpha_{0}}(m_{1})^{-l}

where
\lambda=|\lambda_{1}|
,
r_{1}=\lambda^{k_{\textsf{n}}}
and
m_{1}=\lceil\lambda^{k_{\textsf{n}}}\rceil
as before.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{\log r_{1}}{\log m_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{\log c^{\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{-c^{\prime\prime}}+(-\alpha_{0})\log l+l\log r_{1}}{\log c^{\prime\prime\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{c^{\prime\prime}}+\alpha_{0}\log l+l\log m_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{\log c^{\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{-c^{\prime\prime}}{l}^{-\alpha_{0}}(r_{1})^{l}}{\log c^{\prime\prime\prime}k_{\textsf{n}}^{c^{\prime\prime}}{l}^{\alpha_{0}}(m_{1})^{l}}\leq\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\frac{-\log diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))}{-\log diam(P_{l}(F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}))}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}v_{\textsf{n},l}

by L’H
\hat{\mathrm{o}}
pital’s rule. Letting
n
approach
\infty
, we then get

1=\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log\lambda^{k_{\textsf{n}}}}{\log\lceil\lambda^{k_{\textsf{n}}}\rceil}=\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log r_{1}}{\log m_{1}}\leq\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}v_{\textsf{n},l}.

In a similar manner,
\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}v_{\textsf{n},l}\leq\frac{\log r_{1}}{\log m_{1}},\quad\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}v_{\textsf{n},l}\leq 1
so that

\displaystyle\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}v_{\textsf{n},l}=\frac{\log r_{1}}{\log m_{1}}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}v_{\textsf{n},l}=\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log r_{1}}{\log m_{1}}=1.

(5.70)

By the remarks in

[
7
, p. 41]
,
a box dimension formula can be given as
\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}^{s}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam((S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))}
, where
N_{l}^{s}(\tilde{F})
is the smallest number of sets of diameter at most
diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))
that cover
\tilde{F}
. Then

\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}^{s}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))}\leq\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\frac{-\log N_{l}^{S}(\tilde{F})}{\log diam(S_{{l,k_{\textsf{n}}}}(\tilde{F}))}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}u_{\textsf{n},l}

for all large
n
.
This inequality, (
5.68
), (
5.69
) and (
5.70
) lead us to

\displaystyle\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F}\ \underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}v_{\textsf{n},l}\leq\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}(u_{\textsf{n},l}v_{\textsf{n},l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\textsf{n}\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\overline{dim}_{B}F_{k_{\textsf{n}}}=\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\underline{\lim}}dim_{B}F_{k}.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.7
.

If we prove that
\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}dim_{B}F_{k}\leq\underline{dim}_{B}\tilde{F},
then Proposition
5.6
yields

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}dim_{B}F_{k}=dim_{B}\tilde{F},
i.e. the box dimension of
\tilde{F}
exists. That will be the case. Due to the organization of the paper,
we defer the proof to Section
7
.

\hfill\Box

6
The Hausdorff Dimension

For completeness, we first recall some dimension estimates from
[
22
]
(see Theorem 4.1 therein), which are extensions of Falconer’s bounds

[
9
]
to certain graph directed sets. Besides
handling the difficulties arising from the overlapping pieces of
F
,
another advantage of using those graph directed sets is that they can also be used to represent the boundary of
F
when
\overset{\circ}{F}\neq\emptyset

[
15
]
.

For an invertible matrix
T\in M_{n}({\mathbb{R}})
,
the singular values

\alpha_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
of
T
are
the positive square roots of the eigenvalues of
T^{*}T
, where
T^{*}
is the
adjoint of
T
. Assume that
0<\alpha_{n}\leq\cdots\leq\alpha_{2}\leq\alpha_{1}
. The
singular value function

\phi^{s}(T)
is defined for
0<s\leq n
as

\phi^{s}(T)=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}...\alpha_{m-1}\alpha_{m}^{s-m+1},

(6.71)

where
m
is the integer such that
m-1<s\leq m
. For
s>n
,
it is defined by
|\det(T)|^{\frac{s}{n}}
. Note that every integral self-affine set
F=F(T,A)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be obtained from a graph
(V,E)
as a graph-directed set
[
15
]
,
where
V
is the set of vertices and
E
is the edge set.
We define a unique nonnegative number

u
by

\lim_{\textsf{r}\rightarrow\infty}\left[\sum_{i,j=1}^{\textsf{m}}\sum_{\mathbf{e}\in E_{i,j}^{\textsf{r}}}\phi^{u}(T^{-\textsf{r}})\right]^{\frac{1}{\textsf{r}}}=1,

(6.72)

and another number
v
by

\lim_{\textsf{r}\rightarrow\infty}\left[\min\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\textsf{m}}\sum_{\mathbf{e}\in E_{i,j}^{\textsf{r}}}(\phi^{v}(T^{\textsf{r}}))^{-1}\ \ \bigg|\ \ 1\leq i\leq m\right\}\right]^{\frac{1}{\textsf{r}}}=1,

(6.73)

where
\min
stands for minimum,
\#V=\textsf{m}
and
E_{i,j}^{\textsf{r}}
denotes the
set of directed
r
-paths from vertex
i
to vertex
j
. Then we have the following.

Proposition 6.1
.

[
22
]
For an integral self-affine set
F=F(T,A)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we always have

v\leq{dim}_{H}F\leq u\leq n.

Remark 6.2
.

Note that there are examples such that
{dim}_{H}F
takes the value
u
or
v
.

\hfill\Box

Since
u
and
v
defined by (
6.72
)-(
6.73
) can be approximated by monotonic sequences (see
[
22
]
), Proposition

6.1

can be used for computational purposes and the determination of exceptional self-affine fractals.
We want to improve these results and to obtain
{dim}_{H}F
as a limit.

6.1
Tile Measures: A set-up needed for the Hausdorff dimension

Let
H^{\alpha}
denote the
\alpha
-dimensional Hausdorff measure (see
[
8
]
).
Then we know that

dim_{H}(F)=\inf\{\alpha:H^{\alpha}(F)=0\}=\sup\{\alpha:H^{\alpha}(F)=\infty\}.

Now we express this fact in terms of the mesh cubes of the previous section. We note that the tile measures with respect to tiles have been arisen in our earlier study
[
20
]
. Here we need a very simple type of them, a cube.
Let
F
be an integral self-affine set and
\Gamma=F(M,D)
be the cube in
the proof of Lemma
5.1
.
