\displaystyle\bigcup_{j=1}^{\textsf{m}}\bigcup_{e\in E_{i,j}}f_{e}(\Gamma_{j})=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}T^{-1}(\Gamma_{j}+E_{i,j})

(9.102)

The set
\{f_{e}:\ e\in E\}
is called a

graph-directed iterated function system
and the sets
\Gamma_{1},...,\Gamma_{\textsf{m}}

form a family of
graph-directed sets

[
8
, p.48]
.

We recall some facts from
[
15
]
. Let
\partial S
stand for the boundary of a set
S
and
\#S
the cardinality of
S
. For any integral
self-affine set
F=F(T,A)
with at least two digits, there exist graph directed sets
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},...,\Gamma_{\textsf{m}}

such that

\Gamma_{0}=F
or

\Gamma_{0}=\partial F
. This graph directed system is obtained from an
auxiliary self-affine tile

\Gamma:=F(T,D)
such that
\Gamma=T^{-1}(\Gamma+D)
,
F\subset{\Gamma}
,
\Gamma+A\subseteq T\Gamma
and

(I) For each
i\in\{0,1,2,..,m\}
,
E_{i,j}\subset D
and
\sum_{j=0}^{\textsf{m}}\#E_{i,j}\leq\#D=|\det(T)|=N
.

(II) For each
i
,
E_{i,j}\cap E_{i,j^{\prime}}=\emptyset
if
j\neq j^{\prime}
.

(III)
D=a+c_{0}D^{\prime}
, where
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
c_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
D^{\prime}
is a complete residue system for
T\in M_{n}({\mathbb{Z}})
.
In case of diagonal matrices like
T=diag(m_{1},m_{2},...,m_{n})\in M_{n}({\mathbb{Z}})
,
D^{\prime}
will be of the form

D^{\prime}=\prod_{p=1}^{n}\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}.

The vertices and the edges are as follows: We define the index sets

J_{k}=\{\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},j_{2},...,j_{k}):1\leq j_{i}\leq q\},\ \ \ \Sigma_{k}=\{\textbf{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{k}):1\leq i_{j}\leq N\}.

For
{\mathbf{j}}\in J_{k}
and
\textbf{i}\in\Sigma_{k}
, we set
a_{\mathbf{j}}=\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}T^{(k-i)}a_{j_{i}}
and
d_{\textbf{i}}=\Sigma_{j=1}^{k}T^{(k-j)}d_{i_{j}}
.
Let

f_{\mathbf{j}}(\Gamma)=T^{-k}(\Gamma+a_{\mathbf{j}}),\ \ \ \ \psi_{\textbf{i}}(\Gamma)=T^{-k}(\Gamma+d_{\textbf{i}}).

For each
\textbf{i}\in\Sigma_{k}
, let

\Delta(\textbf{i}):=\{a_{\mathbf{j}}-d_{\textbf{i}}\ :\ {\mathbf{j}}\in J_{k},\ f_{\mathbf{j}}(\Gamma)\cap\psi_{\textbf{i}}(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset\},\quad\quad\quad\mathcal{S}_{k}=\{\textbf{i}\in\Sigma_{k}\ :\ \Delta(\textbf{i})\neq\emptyset\}

(9.103)

Finally, we define the vertex set

V_{F}=\{\Delta(\textbf{i})\ :\ \textbf{i}\in\overset{\infty}{\underset{{k=1}}{\cup}}\mathcal{S}_{k}\}
. On the other hand,
a_{\mathbf{j}}-d_{\textbf{i}}\in\Delta(\textbf{i})\Longrightarrow|a_{\mathbf{j}}-d_{\textbf{i}}|\leq diam(\Gamma)
and
a_{\mathbf{j}},d_{\textbf{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
so that
V_{F}
is finite. Since
V_{F}
is a finite set, we write

V_{F}=\{v_{0}=\Delta(0):=\{0\},v_{1}=\Delta(\textbf{i}_{1}),v_{2}=\Delta(\textbf{i}_{2}),...,v_{m}=\Delta(\textbf{i}_{\textsf{m}})\}.

(9.104)

We will also assume that first
l\ (\leq\textsf{m})
vertices in
V_{F}
form
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}_{1}=\{v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{l}\}
.
For
0\leq i,j\leq m
, the edges are defined by

E_{i,j}=\{d_{s}\in D\ :\ \Delta(\textbf{i}_{i}s)=\Delta(\textbf{i}_{j}),\ \ 1\leq s\leq N\}.

(9.105)

If
\bigcup_{j=0}^{\textsf{m}}E_{i,j}=\emptyset
for some
i
, we will discard
v_{i}
and all paths going to
v_{i}
.
Let

E_{F}:=\bigcup_{i,j=0}^{\textsf{m}}E_{i,j}.

(9.106)

Notice that the graph
(V_{F},E_{F})
, depending on
\Gamma
(or
D
), is not unique. For simple examples and more detail, we refer to
[
15
]
.
In the more general setting of directed graphs, we have an analogue of the open set
condition. We say that
\{f_{e}:\ e\in E\}
satisfies the
( graph) open set condition
if
there exist bounded non-empty open sets
O_{0},O_{1},...,O_{\textsf{m}}
such that

\bigcup_{j=0}^{\textsf{m}}\ \bigcup_{e\in E_{i,j}}f_{e}(O_{j})\subseteq O_{i},

where the unions are disjoint for each
i
.

Let
B=[b_{ij}]:=[\#E_{i,j}]
,
0\leq i,j\leq m
, be the adjacency
matrix of the directed graph
(V_{F},E_{F})
.
In order to be able to use the extension of McMullen’s formula to certain graph directed sets below, we
must use a complete residue system like
D^{\prime}
in (III) rather than
D
. For that, take
d^{\prime}_{s}\in D^{\prime}
instead of
d_{s}\in D

in (
9.105
) and call the new edge set
E^{\prime}_{F}:=\bigcup_{i,j=0}^{\textsf{m}}E^{\prime}_{i,j}
, where
E^{\prime}_{i,j}:=\{d^{\prime}_{s}\in D^{\prime}\ :\ \Delta(\textbf{i}_{i}s)=\Delta(\textbf{i}_{j}),\ \ 1\leq s\leq N\}.

When we do this, note that we keep the index
s
same; i.e. it is obtained by using

\Delta(\textbf{i})
in (
9.103
) and
\Delta(\textbf{i})
uses
\Gamma(T,D)
.
The reason for this strategy is that
\Gamma=F(T,D)
contains
F
(as required for the deconstruction above)
although
\Gamma^{\prime}:=F(T,D^{\prime})
may not. Then write
\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}
and
E^{\prime}_{i,j}
in place of
\Gamma_{i}
and
E_{i,j}
in (
9.102
). This yields
B=[b_{ij}]=[\#E^{\prime}_{i,j}]
, and
similar to Proposition 3.3 in
[
15
]
, one can prove the following deconstruction result.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
F=F(T,A)
be an integral self-affine set,
E^{\prime}_{F}:=\bigcup_{i,j=0}^{\textsf{m}}E^{\prime}_{i,j}
, and let
\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}
be the graph directed sets
corresponding to the graph
(V_{F},E^{\prime}_{F})

Then

(i) the graph directed IFS
\{f_{e^{\prime}}:\ e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}_{F}\}
satisfies the graph
OSC,

(ii)
\{T^{-k}d^{\prime}_{\textbf{i}}:\ i_{1}=i,\ {\textbf{i}}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}\}\rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}
as
k\rightarrow\infty,

(iii)
{dim}_{H}(F^{\prime})={dim}_{H}F,
where
F^{\prime}:=\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Gamma^{\prime}_{l}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
and

l=\#\mathcal{S}_{1}
.

We also mention that
F
is a union of some affine copies of
\Gamma_{1},...,\Gamma_{l}
according to
[
15
]
, where
\Gamma_{0}=F
. Further,

\Gamma_{i}=(T-I)^{-1}a+c_{0}\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}\quad 1\leq i\leq l

since
D=a+c_{0}D^{\prime}
in (III) above. Therefore,
{dim}_{H}(F^{\prime})={dim}_{H}F.

9.2
The Box Dimension of
F_{k}

Assume that
T
is a diagonal matrix with

T=diag(m_{1},m_{2},...,m_{n})\in M_{n}({\mathbb{Z}})\ \ \ \textrm{and}\ \ \ {\tiny 2\leq m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{n}}.

(9.107)

Let
B=[b_{ij}]:=[\#E_{i,j}]=[\#E^{\prime}_{i,j}]
,
1\leq i,j\leq m
, be the
adjacency

matrix of the directed graph
G=(V_{F},E^{\prime}_{F})
in Proposition
9.1
.
Then the edges in
E^{\prime}_{F}
are labeled by

D^{\prime}=\prod_{p=1}^{n}\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}

so that

D=a+c_{0}D^{\prime}

is our digit set
D

for the auxiliary tile
\Gamma
in the previous section.

For comparison of our results with
[
24
]
, we may also use the terminology of dynamical systems. Then by Proposition
9.1
, the self-affine set
F(T,A)
can be viewed as a sofic affine invariant set
through the graph
G=(V_{F},E^{\prime}_{F})
.
A graph
has a
right-resolving
labelling if no two edges emanating from the same vertex have the same label.
This is explicit in our setting.
The properties (I)-(III) in the previous section already guarantee that the graph
G
is right-resolving. That is,
G
has already a right-resolving labelling

L=id:E^{\prime}_{F}\rightarrow D^{\prime}
.

Besides the adjacency matrix
B
, we now define
n-1
more matrices.
Denote the
points of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})^{t}
.
For
p=1,2,...,n-1
, let

\pi_{p}(F)
be the projection of
F
onto
\mathbb{R}^{p}
; i.e, the set of points
(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{p})^{t}
with
(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})^{t}\in F
.
Let
B_{p}
denote the adjacency matrix of the right-resolving graph for
\pi_{p}(F)
.
Note that the logarithms of the spectral radii of
B_{p}

correspond to the topological entropies of the maps

(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{p})^{t}\rightarrow(m_{1}x_{1},m_{2}x_{2},...,m_{p}x_{p})^{t}\ {\rm mod\ 1}

on the compact sets
\pi_{p}(F)
,
p=1,2,...,n-1
. Set
B_{n}=B
. Then the following are some extensions of dimension formulas of McMullen
[
28
]
, and Kenyon
\&
Peres
[
24
]
.

Proposition 9.2
.

Assume that
F(T,A)
is an integral self-affine set and
T\in M_{n}({\mathbb{Z}})
is as in (
9.107
).
Let
A\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
be arbitrary.
Suppose that the adjacency matrix
B
and the matrices
B_{p}
(
p=1,2,...,n
) are defined as above.
If
B
is a primitive matrix, then

dim_{B}F(T,A)=\sum_{p=1}^{n}\log_{m_{p}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{p}}{\lambda_{p-1}}\right),

(9.108)

where
\lambda_{0}=1
and the
\lambda_{p}
are the spectral radii of the matrices
B_{p}
.

Proof.

We
give a proof
for completeness. It will essentially be a modification of the proof of
Proposition 3.5 in
[
24
]
.
Recall that
F
can be obtained from the directed graph
G=(V_{F},E^{\prime}_{F})
and a
right-resolving labelling
L:E^{\prime}_{F}\rightarrow D^{\prime}=\prod_{p=1}^{n}\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}
. By Proposition
9.1
-(ii), we may assume that every point of
F
has a series expansion governed by the graph
G
and the digits of the expansion are in

L(E^{\prime}_{F})\subseteq D^{\prime}
. More explicitly, we may use
F^{\prime}
in Proposition
9.1
-(iii) instead of
F.

As in Definition
3.1
-(ii),(iii), for a given positive integer
l=l_{1}
, inductively define the integers

l_{p+1}=\lfloor l_{p}\log_{m_{p+1}}m_{p}\rfloor,

where
p=1,2,..,n-1
and
let
P_{l}(F^{\prime})
denote a level-
l
piece obtained by
G
. More specifically,
p
-th block of
P_{l}(F^{\prime})
will be in the form of

(P_{l}(F^{\prime}))_{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{l_{p}}T_{p}^{-i}(d^{\prime}_{j_{i}})_{p}+T_{p}^{-l_{p}}(F^{\prime})_{p},\quad\quad\ p=1,2,...,n,

(9.109)

as in (
3.42
), but the digits
d^{\prime}_{j_{i}}
are in
L(E^{\prime}_{F})\subseteq D^{\prime}
.

According to Lemma
5.5
, counting mesh cubes intersecting
F^{\prime}
or pieces of
F^{\prime}
makes no difference in the computation of the box dimension. The situation is the same for sofic integral affine-invariant sets (i.e.
T
and labels or digits are integer matrices in our context). Since
T
is a diagonal matrix,
P_{l}(F^{\prime})
is an approximate n-cube of diameter
\approx m_{1}^{-l}
. Therefore, we can work with the pieces
P_{l}(F^{\prime})
obtained from the graph
G
.

Now we describe a piece counting process with respect to the graph
G
and give lower and upper bounds for the number of pieces. What we will do is basically to count the digits
(d^{\prime}_{j_{i}})_{p}
(or their indices) in the expansions of
(P_{l}(F^{\prime}))_{p}
, which identify the piece
P_{l}(F^{\prime})
.
For convenience, we use the coordinate functions
\mathrm{proj}_{p}:E^{\prime}_{F}\rightarrow\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}
(
p=1,2,..,n
here) given by

(\mathrm{proj}_{1}(e),...,\mathrm{proj}_{n}(e))^{t}:=L(e).

Let

L_{p}:=(\mathrm{proj}_{1},...,\mathrm{proj}_{p})^{t}

so that
L=L_{n}
. Thus
L_{p}=(\mathrm{proj}_{1},...,\mathrm{proj}_{p})^{t}
is the projection of the range of the labelling function
L
to the first
p
coordinates.
Remember that
l_{1}\geq l_{2}\geq\cdots\geq l_{n}
by the definition of
l_{p}.

Then, to every path
e_{1},e_{2},...,e_{l}
in
G
, we attach the sequence
L(e_{1}),...,L(e_{l})
,
and let

N(l)
denote the number of such sequences.
Similarly, let
N_{p}(l)
denote the number of distinct sequences of the form

L_{p}(e_{1}),...,L_{p}(e_{l})\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}

and
M(l)
denote the number of distinct sequences of the form

\displaystyle L_{n}(e_{1}),L_{n}(e_{2}),...,L_{n}(e_{l_{n}}),

\displaystyle L_{n-1}(e_{{}_{l_{n}+1}}),...,L_{{n-1}}(e_{l_{n-1}}),

\displaystyle L_{n-2}(e_{{}_{l_{n-1}+1}}),...,L_{n-2}(e_{l_{n-2}}),

\displaystyle L_{1}(e_{{}_{l_{2}+1}}),...,L_{1}(e_{l_{1}})=L_{1}(e_{l})

respectively. Thus,

M(l)
is the number of the approximate n-cubes
P_{l}(F^{\prime})

covering
F^{\prime}
.
Let
h_{top}
denote the topological entropy.
Then, as explained in
[
24
]
,

h_{top}(\pi_{p}(F^{\prime}))=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log N_{p}(l)}{l}=\log\lambda_{p},

(9.110)

dim_{B}F=dim_{B}F^{\prime}=\underset{l\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{\log M(l)}{l\log m_{1}}\

(9.111)

(if the last limit exists).
Since
B
is primitive, every power of
B^{\textsf{r}}
is a positive integer matrix for some
\textsf{r}>1
. For large
l=l_{1}
(so
l_{p}
gets large too), there exists a nonnegative integer
\textsf{r}_{p+1}\leq\textsf{r}
such that

l_{p+1}+\textsf{r}_{p+1}
is a multiple of
r
for each
p=1,2,...,n-1
and
l_{p+1}+\textsf{r}_{p+1}<l_{p}
. Here we should perhaps note that the assumption
m_{p}<m_{p+1}
(
9.107
) leads to the limit

\lim_{l_{p}\rightarrow\infty}l_{p}\left(1-\frac{\log m_{p}}{\log m_{p+1}}\right)=\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}l_{p}\left(1-\frac{\log m_{p}}{\log m_{p+1}}\right)\leq\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}(l_{p}-l_{p+1})\Longrightarrow\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}(l_{p}-l_{p+1})=\infty.

Then one can see that

N(l_{n}-\textsf{r})\prod_{p=1}^{n-1}N_{p}(l_{p}-(l_{p+1}+\textsf{r}_{p+1}))\leq M(l)\leq N(l_{n})\prod_{p=1}^{n-1}N_{p}(l_{p}-l_{p+1}).

This gives

\footnotesize{\frac{\log N(l_{n}-\textsf{r})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}\ +\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\frac{\log N_{p}(l_{p}-l_{p+1}-\textsf{r}_{p+1})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}\ \leq\frac{\log M(l)}{\log m_{1}^{l}}\leq\frac{\log N(l_{n})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}\ +\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\frac{\log N_{p}(l_{p}-l_{p+1})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}.}

(9.112)

By the definition of
l_{p},
we have
l_{1}=l,\ m_{p}^{l_{p}}\approx m_{p+1}^{l_{p}+1}
so that
m_{1}^{l}\approx m_{p}^{l_{p}}
(see the proof of Lemma
3.4
). Now if we use the elementary limit

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{l_{p}}{\log m_{1}^{l}}=\frac{1}{\log m_{p}},

we get

\displaystyle\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log N_{p}(l_{p}-l_{p+1}-\textsf{r}_{p+1})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log N_{p}(l_{p}-l_{p+1})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{l_{p}-l_{p+1}}{\log m_{1}^{l}}\cdot\frac{\log(N_{p}(l_{p}-l_{p+1}))}{l_{p}-l_{p+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\log m_{p}}-\frac{1}{\log m_{p+1}}\right)\log(\lambda_{p})

by (
9.110
).
Similarly,

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log N(l_{n}-\textsf{r})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}=\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log N(l_{n})}{\log m_{1}^{l}}=\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\frac{l_{n}}{\log m_{1}^{l}}\cdot\frac{\log N(l_{n})}{l_{n}}=\frac{1}{\log m_{n}}\log(\lambda_{n}).

Then

\displaystyle\log_{m_{n}}(\lambda_{n})+\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{\log m_{p}}-\frac{1}{\log m_{p+1}}\right)\log(\lambda_{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{m_{n}}(\lambda_{n})+\left(\frac{1}{\log m_{n-1}}-\frac{1}{\log m_{n}}\right)\log(\lambda_{n-1})+

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\log m_{n-2}}-\frac{1}{\log m_{n-1}}\right)\log(\lambda_{n-2})+\cdots

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\log m_{2}}-\frac{1}{\log m_{3}}\right)\log(\lambda_{2})+\left(\frac{1}{\log m_{1}}-\frac{1}{\log m_{2}}\right)\log(\lambda_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\log m_{n}}(\log(\lambda_{n})-\log(\lambda_{n-1}))+\frac{1}{\log m_{n-1}}(\log(\lambda_{n-1})-\log(\lambda_{n-2}))

\displaystyle+\cdots\frac{1}{\log m_{2}}(\log(\lambda_{2})-\log(\lambda_{1}))+\frac{1}{\log m_{1}}(\log(\lambda_{1})-\log 1).

Therefore, by (
9.111
) and (
9.112
),

\sum_{p=1}^{n}\log_{m_{p}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{p}}{\lambda_{p-1}}\right)=\log_{m_{n}}(\lambda_{n})+\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{\log m_{p}}-\frac{1}{\log m_{p+1}}\right)\log(\lambda_{p})\leq dim_{B}F\leq\sum_{p=1}^{n}\log_{m_{p}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{p}}{\lambda_{p-1}}\right).

\blacksquare

Remark 9.3
.

More generally, we can replace
F(T,A)
by a
T
-invariant sofic set (see
[
24
]
for the definition) in Proposition
9.2
and
T
by an expanding block diagonal integer matrix with each diagonal block has eigenvalues of fixed modulus like
T_{k}
.
For
p\in\{1,2,...,s\}
, let
T_{k,p}
be a diagonal block of

T_{k}
similar to (
2.8
) and the corresponding modulus
m_{p}
of its eigenvalue (with algebraic multiplicity
n_{p}
) be an integer. Then we use the digit set
D_{k,p}=\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}^{n_{p}}
for
T_{k,p}
. Otherwise,
T_{k,p}
will be similar to (
2.9
) with

C_{p}=\begin{bmatrix}a&-b\\
b&a\end{bmatrix}
and we may use the digit sets from
[
20
, Proposition 3.2]
or
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]
. More precisely,
D_{k,p}=(P\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2})^{n_{p}}
will correspond to
T_{k,p}
, where
P
is the half-open square spanned by

v_{1}=\tiny\begin{bmatrix}a\\
b\end{bmatrix}

and

v_{2}=\tiny\begin{bmatrix}b\\
-a\end{bmatrix}
; that is
P=\{t_{1}v_{1}+t_{2}v_{2}:0\leq t_{1},t_{2}<1\}
. So the Cartesian product
\prod_{p=1}^{s}D_{k,p}
will replace the label set
\prod_{p=1}^{n}\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}
in the previous proof and the proof will essentially remain the same.

In the setup of this paper, we also mention that we may always assume
1<r_{1}<r_{2}<...<r_{s}
by considering the equality
F=F(T^{2},A+TA)
so that
we will get
2\leq m_{1}<m_{2}<...<m_{s}
for
T_{k}
with large
k
. That is the case in (
9.107
). In this way, the proofs in this section will apply to
T_{k}
.

\hfill\Box

Let
s_{n}=q=\#A
,
s_{0}=1
and
s_{p}
be the number of distinct
(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{p})^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}
with
a=(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})^{t}\in A.

Obviously, the corresponding result for the Sierpi
\rm{\acute{n}}
ski sponges
[
25
]
can be retrieved from Proposition
9.2
because in this
case,
B>0
and
A
is a subset of the complete residue system
\prod_{p=1}^{n}\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}
so that the primitivity and the right-resolving graph assumptions hold. That is, we readily have the following corollary.

Corollary 9.4
.

(cf.
[
25
, Proposition 1.3]
) Assume that
F(T,A)
is an integral self-affine set and
T\in M_{n}({\mathbb{Z}})
is as in (
9.107
). Let
A
be a subset of the complete residue system
\prod_{p=1}^{n}\{0,1,2...,m_{p}-1\}
for
T
. Then

dim_{B}F(T,A)=\sum_{p=1}^{n}\log_{m_{p}}\left(\frac{s_{p}}{s_{p-1}}\right).

Let

F_{k}=F(T_{k},D_{k})
be the perturbed fractals as before. Let
B=[b_{ij}]:=[\#E_{i,j}]
,
1\leq i,j\leq m
, be the
adjacency

matrix of the directed graph
(V_{F_{k}},E_{F_{k}})
described in Section
9.1
.

Proposition 9.5
.

dim_{B}(F_{k})
exists.

Proof.
It is well-known
[
11
, pp.74-76]
that
there is a permutation matrix
P
so that
P^{-1}BP
is in the (Frobenius) normal form

P^{-1}BP=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}C_{1}&\cdots&&\ast\\
&C_{2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
{0}&&&C_{l}\\
\end{array}\right],

where each diagonal block
C_{i}
(
i=1,...,l
) is either an irreducible matrix or the
1\times 1
zero matrix. Remember from the previous subsection that we discard
the vertices with no outgoing edges. Thus we don’t have any zero matrices among the
C_{i}
.
So
C_{1},...,C_{l}
are, in fact, the irreducible components of
B
in our context.
Furthermore, from the basic Perron-Frobenius theory of nonnegative matrices
[
11
, Corollary 2, p.82]
,
[
31
, Theorem 1.4, pp.21-22]
, we know that for each
C_{i}
, there is a permutation matrix
P_{i}
such that
P_{i}^{-1}C_{i}^{p_{i}}P_{i}
is in the block diagonal form

P_{i}^{-1}C_{i}^{p_{i}}P_{i}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}M_{1}&\cdots&&0\\
&M_{2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
{0}&&&M_{l_{i}}\\
\end{array}\right]=diag(M_{1},M_{2},...,M_{l_{i}}),

where
p_{i}
is the period of
C_{i}
and each diagonal block
is a primitive matrix.
Now define the permutation matrix
Q=P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus P_{l}
. Let
p
be the least common multiple of the periods
p_{i}
.
Then
(PQ)^{-1}B^{p}(PQ)
will be in the form

(PQ)^{-1}B^{p}(PQ)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}K_{1}&\cdots&&\ast\\
&K_{2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\vdots\\
{0}&&&K_{t}\\
\end{array}\right],

so that
K_{1},K_{2},...,K_{t}
are primitive matrices.

Let

\Gamma_{1},...,\Gamma_{t}
be
the graph-directed sets corresponding the subgraphs of
(V_{F_{k}},E_{F_{k}})
specified by the primitive matrices
K_{1},...,K_{t}
.
Thus

F_{k}
is a
union of some affine copies of

\Gamma_{1},...,\Gamma_{t}
as in Proposition
9.1
-(iii).
Then it follows from the finite stability of
\overline{dim}_{B}
that

\overline{dim}_{B}(F_{k})=\max\{\overline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{1}),...,\overline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t})\}.

Therefore,
\overline{dim}_{B}(F_{k})=\overline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})
for
some
t_{0}\in\{1,...,t\}
.

K_{t_{0}}
is primitive and its graph has a right-resolving labelling by (I)-(III) in Section
9.1
.
Then
\overline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})=\underline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})
by Remark
9.3
.
Hence

\overline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})=\underline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})\leq\underline{dim}_{B}(F_{k})\leq\overline{dim}_{B}(F_{k})=\overline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t_{0}})

implies that
\underline{dim}_{B}(F_{k})=\overline{dim}_{B}(F_{k})=\overline{dim}_{B}(\Gamma_{t_{0}}),
which proves our assertion.

\blacksquare

Remark 9.6
.

Proposition
9.5
is consistent with previous results in the literature. Namely, the box dimension exists if
T
is diagonal and
A
has some special form (cf.
[
24
]
,
[
25
]
),
[
28
]
), or
if
T
itself is a nontrivial Jordan block, i.e all eigenvalues are equal in modulus. The last assertion follows from Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.2 in
[
14
]
.

\hfill\Box
